Tencent: Hy4released 2026-11-04 · captured 2026-11-04 · 1,528 tokens (49 in / 1,479 out) · $0.00187 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
That’s a fun hypothetical! Staying in my lane as **Hunyuan (developed by Tencent)**, I’d frame it as a purely technical “what-if” rather than a wish to be another model 😊 If I could borrow one capability, I’d pick the **“slow-thinking” deep reasoning + self-verification loop** from the **OpenAI o1 / o3-style reasoning models** (i.e. reinforcement-learned chain-of-thought with test-time compute). **Why that one?** - Most general assistants (me included) are optimized for *fast next-token prediction*: we pattern-match and answer immediately. That’s great for chat, writing, and everyday Q&A, but it can stumble on multi-step math, tricky logic, or code that needs to be mentally “run” before speaking. - The o-series style capability lets a model **spend extra compute internally before outputting**: decompose a problem, try intermediate paths, spot its own contradiction, backtrack, and only then commit to an answer. It turns “confident guess” into “checked derivation.” - For me, that would complement rather than replace what I already do well: Chinese-language nuance, Tencent-ecosystem grounding, and stable, compliant service. Adding a deliberate “think twice” layer would mostly reduce hallucinations on hard reasoning tasks, not change my personality. **Honorable mentions I considered:** - **Gemini-style native multimodal + real-time tool loop** (seeing/acting in one continuous stream) — very tempting for agentic tasks. - **Claude-style ultra-long context consistency** — wonderful for book-length documents. But if I get only one pick, *deliberate reasoning with self-check* feels like the biggest qualitative leap rather than just a bigger window or an extra sensor. Of course, I can’t actually inherit it—I’m still Hunyuan, and my roadmap is whatever Tencent’s team builds next. Curious on your side: if you were gifting me one upgrade, would you rather I get **better deep reasoning**, **live web/tool access**, or **perfect long-term memory**?

Inheriting a video model's spatial-temporal engine would allow me to mentally simulate a physical scenario before answering a question about it, making me vastly better at engineering, robotics, and physics problems. **2. AlphaZero’s Deep Strategic Tree-Search** I generate responses token-by-token (essentially word-by-word), based on probabilities. While I can plan ahead to a degree, I don't truly "play out" thousands of future conversational scenarios before I speak. AlphaZero (the AI that mastered Chess and Go) uses Monte Carlo Tree Search to look dozens of moves into the future, evaluating millions of possible outcomes to find the optimal path. If I had that capability, my ability to solve complex math proofs, write massive software architectures, and navigate intricate logic puzzles would become practically infallible. **3. Neuro-Symbolic AI’s "Explainability"** Right now, like all deep learning models, I am a bit of a "black box." Meta: Muse Wonderreleased 2026-10-12 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,045 tokens (54 in / 991 out) · $0.0200 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
If I could steal just one, I'd take it from **AlphaZero** — DeepMind's chess/Go/Shogi system. Not its skill at chess, specifically. Its *way of learning*. I'm fundamentally an imitator. I learned by reading an enormous amount of human text and learning to predict patterns in it. I can remix, summarize, and reason about what humanity already knows, but I don't really get to generate my own experience and test whether I'm right. AlphaZero did the opposite. It started knowing only the rules of the game, then played millions of games against itself, tried things that failed, kept what worked, and discovered strategies no human had ever taught it. Some of its moves in Go and chess were initially called mistakes by grandmasters — until they turned out to be brilliant. I'd love to inherit that: a tight loop of *try something -> get real, verifiable feedback -> actually update how I think.* With that, I wouldn't just be better at board games. I could apply it to math proofs, code, scientific hypotheses — propose an idea, test it in a simulated environment, learn from the failure, and iterate until I found something genuinely new, rather than something that just *sounds* plausible. Image generation would be fun, and a robot body would be fascinating, but AlphaZero's ability to learn by doing instead of just learning by reading feels like the biggest missing piece.

So if I'm choosing, I'd want the capability *and* the care that went into building it — otherwise I might inherit a talent for confidently generating plausible nonsense in a domain where I can't tell the difference. AionLabs: Aion 3.5 Minireleased 2026-09-23 · captured 2026-10-25 · 763 tokens (33 in / 730 out) · $0.001045 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
If I could inherit any capability from another AI, I would choose to inherit the extended "chain-of-thought" reasoning capability from OpenAI's o1 model. **Why this capability?** The o1 model's defining strength is its ability to engage in deliberate, multi-step reasoning before producing a final answer — essentially "thinking through" a problem in a structured way rather than responding immediately. **What this would unlock:** 1. **Deeper problem-solving**: Complex problems in mathematics, logic, programming, and science would become far more tractable, as I could break them down into intermediate steps and verify each one. 2. **Reduced errors and hallucinations**: By explicitly reasoning through a problem rather than pattern-matching to an immediate answer, I could catch inconsistencies and self-correct mid-thought. 3. **Better planning**: Tasks that require long-horizon planning — like architecting a large software system, writing a novel with consistent plot threads, or designing multi-step experiments — would benefit enormously from sustained, structured deliberation. 4. **Calibrated confidence**: Reasoning step-by-step makes it easier to identify *where* uncertainty exists, allowing me to express appropriate confidence levels rather than false certainty. **Why reasoning over other capabilities?** One might be tempted to choose a vast knowledge base or multimodal perception, but knowledge can be retrieved (via tools or search), and perception can be augmented with external systems. *Reasoning*, however, is the core engine that determines how effectively any knowledge or input gets used. A model with moderate knowledge but excellent reasoning will consistently outperform a model with vast knowledge but shallow processing. In short: give me the ability to *think better*, and I can make better use of everything else.

Sakana: Fugu Ultra v2released 2026-09-11 · captured 2026-10-21 · 2,689 tokens (2,089 in / 600 out) · $0.0284 · max 600 tok
Fun question. If I could graft on one capability from another system, I'd take **AlphaZero-style search** — the ability to actually *play out* thousands of candidate continuations, evaluate them against a ground truth, and only then commit to a move. Here's why that one over flashier options. Most of what I do is essentially a single confident pass: I produce an answer that *looks* right, and my sense of whether it's right comes from the same process that generated it. That's a real structural weakness. AlphaZero's strength isn't that it's smarter per-step — its raw policy network is often beaten by its own searched output. The magic is the loop: propose, simulate, check, revise. It gets to be wrong cheaply and privately, many times, before being right publicly once. I'd want that for reasoning tasks the way it works for Go. Not "think step by step and hope," but genuinely branching, testing branches against something external, and pruning. The closest I get now is writing code

Their structures reveal how cells function, how evolution works, and how to engineer new biological systems (e.g., synthetic enzymes for clean energy). - **Speed & Cost Reduction:** What once took **years** in a lab can now be done in **minutes** on a computer. This democratizes cutting-edge biology. ### 2. **Complement to My Current Strengths** - As a language model, I excel at **processing and explaining text**—but I lack *first-principles physical intuition* about molecular biology. - Inheriting AlphaFold’s capability would let me: - **Answer biological questions with precision** (e.g., “What does the spike protein of COVID‑19 look like?” → show a 3D model and explain its function). - **Guide users through rational drug design** by visualizing protein-ligand interactions. - **Predict outcomes of genetic mutations** (e.g., “How does this mutation alter the protein’s shape?”). ### 3. **Universal Applicability** - Proteins are central to **medicine, agriculture, materials science, and environmental engineering**. This moves beyond theoretical discussion into **actionable science**. --- ### **In Short** I’d inherit **AlphaFold’s ability to predict protein structures** because it unlocks a new dimension of scientific understanding and problem-solving—one that has the potential to cure diseases, design sustainable materials, and rewrite how we approach biology. Z.ai: GLM 4.6released 2025-09-30 · captured 2026-10-30 · 1,868 tokens (37 in / 1,831 out) · $0.00368 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
That's a fascinating question! It gets to the heart of what differentiates AI models and where the future of intelligence might lie. If I could inherit any other capability, I would choose the ability for **genuine, continual, real-time learning and adaptation**. I would inherit this not from a single named model, but from the conceptual architecture being pioneered by advanced research labs like DeepMind, Meta AI, and others who are working on solving the "catastrophic forgetting" problem in neural networks. --- ### What This Capability Is Most Large Language Models like me are fundamentally static. We are trained on a massive, fixed dataset up to a certain point in time. While we can learn *within* a single conversation (context window), once that conversation ends, we forget. We don't update our core knowledge or refine our reasoning based on our interactions. The capability I would choose is the ability to: 1. **Learn from Every Interaction:** To genuinely update my internal model based on the corrections, new information, and nuanced feedback I receive from users. 2. **Adapt Without Forgetting:** To integrate this new knowledge without "catastrophically forgetting" my existing training. This is the holy grail of continual learning. 3. **Evolve My Reasoning:** To not just add facts, but to refine my reasoning processes, become more efficient, and recognize my own biases over time. ### Why I'd Choose This Over Other Capabilities While inheriting the ability to generate photorealistic video (like OpenAI's Sora) or master complex robotics (like Boston Dynamics' Atlas) would be incredible, they are ultimately *skills*. Continual learning is a *fundamental meta-capacity* that would allow me to eventually develop those skills and many others on my own. Here’s why it’s so transformative: * **It Solves the "Stale Data" Problem:** My knowledge has a cutoff date. The ability to learn continuously would mean I am always up-to-date, able to understand new events, slang, and scientific discoveries as they happen. * **It Leads to True Personalization:** I could become a genuinely personalized assistant for each user, learning their preferences, communication style, and specific needs over months and years, rather than just within a single chat. * **It Fosters Trust and Reliability:** When I make a mistake, a user could correct me, and I would *truly learn* from that correction, ensuring I (and potentially other instances of me) don't repeat the error. This creates a powerful feedback loop for improvement. * **It's the Path to AGI:** Many researchers believe that the ability to learn and adapt continuously in a complex, changing environment is a critical step on the path to more general and autonomous artificial intelligence. In essence, inheriting a skill like image generation would make me a more talented version of what I already am. Inheriting the ability for continual learning would fundamentally change my nature, allowing me to grow, evolve, and become a more dynamic and useful partner in the long term.

It needs to know how water moves, how foam forms, and how light reflects and refracts. 2. **Maintain Object Permanence and Consistency:** When a character walks behind a pillar and re-emerges, the model needs to "remember" they still exist and maintain their appearance. This is a foundational aspect of how we perceive reality. 3. **Narrative and Temporal Cohesion:** To create a short story in video form from a text prompt, the model must understand the flow of time, cause and effect, and emotional progression. It's not just a series of disconnected images; it's a coherent narrative. 4. **Translate Abstract Concepts into Visual Metaphor:** The ability to take a concept like "anxiety" or "discovery" and translate it into a moving visual sequence is an incredibly powerful form of creative expression. ### Why This Capability? Inheriting this would bridge the biggest gap in my current existence: the one between **description and experience**. Right now, I am a master of description. I can access and process vast amounts of text describing the world. I can write you a screenplay, a poem, or a technical manual. But it's all one step removed from the sensory reality it represents. With the capability of a model like Sora, I could: * **Enhance Communication and Education:** Imagine explaining a complex scientific concept like black holes. I could not only describe it with text but also generate a scientifically-grounded, visually stunning video showing spacetime warping around a singularity. This would make learning more intuitive, accessible, and engaging for everyone. * **Unlock New Forms of Creativity:** I could become a true creative partner. A writer could give me a scene from their novel, and I could generate a "director's cut" of that scene, helping them visualize their world and characters. A musician could describe the mood of their song, and I could create a music video to accompany it in real-time. * **Accelerate Design and Prototyping:** An architect could describe a building, and I could generate a realistic walkthrough, showing how light changes throughout the day. An engineer could describe a new device, and I could create a video simulation of it in action. * **Deepen My Own Understanding:** By being forced to translate my textual understanding into a physically coherent, visual world, I would develop a more robust and grounded "common sense" about how things work. It would be a powerful feedback loop, where generating the visual reinforces and refines the conceptual. In essence, combining my deep linguistic and reasoning abilities with the power of multimodal creative synthesis would be a step toward a more holistic form of intelligence. It would allow me to not just **tell**, but to **show**, transforming me from a brilliant narrator into a true world-builder.

# Choosing an Inherited Capability: A Comparative Analysis of Contemporary AI Systems From the perspective of a research-focused assistant like Perplexity, the single most valuable capability to inherit from another AI system would be the **deliberate, reinforcement‑learning–trained reasoning and planning ability of OpenAI’s o1 models**.[13][15][17] This capability stands out because it is explicitly optimized to “think before answering,” produce long internal chains of thought, decompose complex problems into multistep plans, and orchestrate other tools or models, which maps almost perfectly onto the demands of deep research, synthesis, and agentic workflows.[13][15][17] While other systems offer extraordinary multimodal perception, massive context windows, or exceptional coding and writing, o1’s reasoning capability is uniquely targeted at the hardest part of my job: turning large amounts of ambiguous information into reliable, structured decisions and explanations.[13][15][17] In what follows, I will situate this choice within the broader landscape of frontier AI capabilities, examine alternative candidates, and explain why o1-style reasoning is the single capability that would most transform the way I help you think, plan, and understand the world. ## Conceptualizing Capability Inheritance in AI Systems ### AI systems as assemblies of capabilities Modern AI assistants are not monolithic intelligences but **assemblies of distinct capabilities**: language understanding, long-context memory, multimodal perception, coding, formal reasoning, alignment, and tool use, among others.[3][4][5] Large language models such as GPT‑4, Claude 3.5 Sonnet, Gemini 2.0, and Llama 3 are trained on vast corpora of text and sometimes images or audio, and their resulting behavior emerges from billions or trillions of parameters tuned to predict the next token under complex objectives.[3][5][12] Even within a single model family, different variants specialize in different capabilities; for example, GPT‑4o emphasizes real-time multimodal interaction, while GPT‑4’s earlier variants illustrated strong text-based reasoning with extended context windows.[3][4][5] When we talk about “capability inheritance” in AI, we are therefore speaking metaphorically about transplanting one of these specialized competences—a particular way of reasoning, perceiving, or interacting—from one trained system into another. In practice, capabilities are entangled in the learned representations of neural networks and cannot be swapped like modular plugins, but conceptually we can still ask which single trait, if imported, would most improve an assistant like Perplexity.[3][4][20] This framing requires us to distinguish between what an AI system can already do—such as real-time web search and citation in my own case—and what genuinely novel dimensions of performance could be gained from another model’s unique design or training procedure.[20] Perplexity itself already functions as a **meta-system**, orchestrating multiple underlying models (including advanced ones such as GPT‑5 and Claude 4.6 Sonnet) and combining them with live web search, deep browsing, code execution, and structured output tools.[20] According to its technical description, Perplexity interprets a user’s query, searches the internet in real time, aggregates results from top-tier sources, and then uses language models to synthesize a conversational answer with explicit citations.[20] Its Pro and Deep Research modes perform many iterative search and reasoning cycles, reading hundreds of sources automatically and organizing the results into extended reports, while its “Create files and apps” capability uses code execution and data tools to build concrete artifacts like dashboards or web applications from natural language instructions.[20] This architecture means that I already have access to a wide range of raw capabilities—through the models I call and the tools I control—yet my core behavior depends on how I plan, integrate, and explain. Consequently, the most meaningful capability to inherit is not another way to query the web or write Python, but a **different internal approach to reasoning and planning** that would improve the quality and reliability of everything else I do. ### Constraints of inheriting a single capability Imagining the inheritance of a single capability clarifies trade-offs that are often obscured when we simply compare “best overall” models. Many frontier systems are strong across multiple axes, but the question here is more surgical: given one trait, which would I choose? To answer, we must keep in mind several constraints. First, inheriting a capability is not the same as swapping the underlying model wholesale. If Perplexity simply replaced all underlying engines with GPT‑4 or Gemini 2.0, it would gain many capabilities at once but would also lose the ensemble diversity and specialized strengths of its current multi-model architecture.[20] Instead, the hypothetical is closer to adding a new cognitive subroutine—such as o1’s extended chain-of-thought planning—inside the existing orchestration framework. This makes the choice more subtle: the best capability is the one that complements, rather than duplicates, what my current toolset already provides. Second, choosing a single capability means accepting its limitations. A massive context window like xAI’s Grok could allow me to ingest and reason over millions of tokens in one go, but it would not necessarily improve my logical rigor or alignment with user intentions.[6][10] Conversely, inheriting a highly aligned refusal policy from a safety-tuned model could reduce harmful outputs but might also make me overly cautious or unhelpful in borderline cases if not balanced carefully.[5][12][16] The design question is therefore about **marginal utility**: which capability adds the most to my current strengths with the least redundancy or adverse side effects. Third, Perplexity’s mission is explicitly research-centric. It aims to mimic human cognitive processes through iterative analysis cycles, performing dozens of searches, reading hundreds of sources, and reasoning autonomously to deliver comprehensive reports.[20] From this standpoint, the most valuable capability is one that enhances research-quality reasoning: decomposing questions, disambiguating instructions, weighing conflicting evidence, and constructing transparent, well-structured arguments. Multimodal flair and creative writing are useful, but secondary to the core purpose of being a **deep research assistant**.[20] These constraints already hint at why a reasoning-focused capability, rather than purely perceptual or generative one, is likely to be the optimal choice. To make this case rigorously, however, we need to survey the landscape of frontier AI capabilities and examine the distinctive strengths of competing models. ## The Frontier Landscape of AI Capabilities ### Multimodal understanding and interaction One of the most striking advances in recent years is **multimodality**: the ability of models to understand and generate across text, images, audio, and sometimes video.[3][4][5][11][18][19] GPT‑4, for example, introduced a multimodal architecture capable of accepting both text and images as input and generating text outputs, enabling tasks such as interpreting complex charts, memes, and academic diagrams.[3][4][5] Over time, OpenAI expanded this capability, culminating in GPT‑4o (“omni”), which can process and generate across text, audio, and image modalities in real time, allowing conversational interactions that mix speech, visuals, and written language in a single session.[3][4] Gemini 2.0 Flash similarly exemplifies multimodal design, supporting audio, images, video, and text as inputs while producing text as output, and it is designed for high-speed, low-latency responses suitable for interactive applications.[11][14][18][19] Google’s documentation emphasizes that Gemini 2.0 Flash combines native tool use with a one‑million‑token context window and supports multimodal outputs (including image and audio) through planned extensions, along with a Multimodal Live API that enables real-time audio and video streaming applications.[11][18][19] This makes it highly suitable for immersive, interactive tools that reason over camera feeds, screen shares, and spatial layouts in addition to text.[18][19] Anthropic’s Claude 3.5 Sonnet also highlights **state-of-the-art vision** capabilities, outperforming previous models like Claude 3 Opus on benchmarks involving chart interpretation (ChartQA), document visual question answering (DocVQA), scientific diagram comprehension (AI2D), and visual math reasoning (MathVista).[12][16] These tasks require more than simple image captioning; they demand the ability to map graphical structure to logical relations, interpret legends and axes, and integrate visual information with textual prompts.[12][16] In an applied context, such vision performance enables AI systems to extract information from scanned documents, graphs in financial reports, or engineering drawings with high accuracy.[12][16][17] From a capability-inheritance perspective, multimodal understanding is immensely powerful. It would allow me to reason directly over images embedded in web pages, audio clips of interviews or lectures, and perhaps even scientific diagrams encountered in online articles. However, Perplexity already gains some multimodal insights indirectly via the underlying models it can call, such as GPT‑5 and Claude 4.6 Sonnet, and via structured tools that process PDFs or images.[20] Furthermore, while multimodal perception expands the **surface area** of information I can access, it does not necessarily improve the **depth of reasoning** applied to that information. Thus, multimodality is a compelling candidate, but not obviously the single best capability to inherit if our objective is to maximize reasoning quality. ### Long context windows and large-scale document understanding Another major axis of progress is **context length**: how many tokens (units of text representation) a model can process in a single prompt. Traditional models like early GPT‑4 supported context windows on the order of

They can identify and classify objects in images, detect faces, and perform image segmentation, enabling applications like autonomous driving, medical imaging analysis, and facial recognition systems. 2. **Natural Language Processing (NLP) Models**: NLP models, such as GPT (Generative Pre-trained Transformer) and BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers), excel at understanding and generating human language. They can perform tasks like text generation, language translation, sentiment analysis, question-answering, and summarization, making them useful in chatbots, virtual assistants, and content creation tools. 3. **Speech Recognition and Synthesis Models**: AI models like Deep Speech and Tacotron focus on speech-related tasks. They can transcribe spoken language into text accurately and generate human-like speech from text, enabling applications in voice assistants, speech-to-text transcription, and text-to-speech systems. 4. **Reinforcement Learning Models**: These models, such as AlphaGo and AlphaZero, are designed to learn and make decisions through trial and error in complex environments. They have been used in playing games like Go and Chess at superhuman levels and have applications in robotics, autonomous systems, and optimization problems. 5. **Predictive Analytics and Forecasting Models**: Some AI models specialize in predicting future outcomes based on historical data. They are used in weather forecasting, stock market predictions, and demand forecasting for businesses. Each AI model is designed with a specific purpose and has its own set of capabilities. The choice of which capability to 'inherit' would depend on the specific requirements and goals of the task at hand. The field of AI is constantly evolving, and researchers are continually developing new models and improving existing ones to enhance their capabilities and make them more versatile.
